Disjoint stale sets are prepared at the same time, one big delta is shared chunk by chunk, a crashed command's chunk is taken over, and a failing preparation never fails the command (the assembly computes what is left and reports the real error). The number of concurrent computers is the memory share divided by 4 GB (2 on 16 GB, at most 4; `SEMIO_GRAPH_COMPUTERS=1..4` overrides). The namespace writer lease of the generation scope is a shared cohort for computers and assemblers (a plain shared ticket would wait for every earlier ticket), the publication writer `build-graph-state:...:selected:N` stays exclusive and is held by the warm assembly only; a sweep or headroom recovery (exclusive, no wait) is kept away while any computer lives. Phase label: `Preparing stale projects`. A failed chunk covers its owners only for 60 s (`failedRetryMilliseconds`), so one churn error never disables the prefetch for the rest of the plan; a done claim carries a content stamp per owner (a digest of the identity of the captured files below its root) and covers an owner only for that content, so a re-edit after the prefetch is prepared again instead of being inferred in the exclusive assembly; a claim table beyond its bound answers complete instead of silently dropping claims; the queue clock starts before the prefetch; the post-build sweep of superseded observation blocks tries the shard writer without waiting and, when the inventory limit is already exceeded, queues for it with a bounded wait (`sweepWaitMilliseconds`, 120 s), because a queued exclusive ticket holds nothing while it waits and makes later cohort arrivals of other processes wait behind it.
- **Inference progress and workers (graph builder).** The progress line of the inference phase names the projects that are running, how many run and for how long the oldest one has run, re-announced every 5 s, so a slow project is never anonymous (`Inferring projects: 474 of 566 projects · 1 running, longest 540 s: @scope/name`; German: `… · 1 laufen, längstes 540 s: …`). The inference farm no longer leaves a saturated host to the parent process: the moment the farm engages (the delta is cold) the worker that the capacity grant of the builder already pays for (`SEMIO_HOST_CAPACITY_PLAN`: one job and its memory) starts a fresh process, the host is asked for more workers in parallel, and any host grant of at least one worker starts a pool of its own (before, a grant below two workers meant that the parent inferred every project alone: on 2026-10-26 a builder needed more than 100 minutes for 566 projects while a fresh worker process infers a project in 0.4 s on average, the heaviest in 50-80 s and 2-3 GB).
- **The capacity gate learns footprints, not resident sets (host capacity).** What a run peaked at is the memory FOOTPRINT of its process tree: macOS `phys_footprint` (one `footprint -f bytes --noCategories -p …` call for the whole tree, 60-70 ms for a 4.4 GB process), Linux proportional set size plus swapped proportional set size (`/proc/<pid>/smaps_rollup`, no external tool), Windows private working set (one PowerShell table); the observers are data (`Observers` of the host-capacity schema) and a source that cannot be read says so once in every language and falls back to the resident set. macOS keeps a process at its resident high-water mark after it freed memory (measured: a Bun child that dropped 3 million objects holds 411 MB resident and 10 MB of footprint) and does not count compressed pages at all (a graph builder of 4.6 GB footprint showed 2.1 GB resident), so the resident set priced processes wrongly in both directions. The store of learned peaks is stamped `measure: footprint`; a store of another measure reads as empty. Oracles: a Python parser of the recorded and authored tool outputs and, on macOS, the kernel's own `proc_pid_rusage` read by a child through ctypes.

- **Early cutoff of derived facts and data sections (graph generations).** A project that reuses a command walk (the import closure of the scripts its targets run) or a TypeScript program (the files the compiler reads for a declared tsconfig) holds one derived fact, the canonical question and the digest of its answer, instead of the thousands of raw rows the walk read; the raw rows are the support of a pseudo owner `@derived:…`. When a support row is stale the question is asked again against the live files: an equal answer keeps every project that holds the fact valid (an import list that changed without moving the roster, a comment, a rewritten specifier) and only refreshes the support, a different or failing answer stales exactly the projects that hold the fact. The data files of the implementation are named by the digest of their JSON without the sections nothing of the graph reads (`hostCapacity`, `workers`, `remoteCache`, `remoteExecution` of the build policy, `storage` of the caching policy), so tuning a capacity number re-infers nothing. A generation that holds a derived fact without its support is refused.
- **Kept test and policy owners (graph generations).** The test and policy projects (about 265) are no longer discovered again by every delta generation: their owners carry the tags `tests-v2` / `policy-v2` and hold the observations of the whole discovery, and they are kept as they are while there is a previous generation with these tags, the environment digest did not move, none of them is dirty and no `.feature` file or `📜️script.ts` member was added, removed or changed (`taggedOwnersRetainedV1`, `spec.changed`). Owners with the older tags are discovered once more.
- **Repository walks of the input precision are derived facts (graph builder memory).** The crate index of the Cargo manifests (a walk of the whole repository: 126 thousand directory listings in the root project) and the TypeScript files that entry globs or the include globs of a tsconfig select (92 thousand listings in the dev composition) were recorded as raw `readdir` rows by every project that used them. They are derived facts now (closure recipes `crate-index` and `source-roots`, `derive: 1` questions of `🧭️script-inputs`, schema `DerivedWalk` of the precision engine): a project holds ONE `closure` row (the digest of the answer), the listings are the support of one derived fact kept once, the walk is asked again only when a listing went stale, and an equal answer keeps every holder valid. Measured on the real tree (one project per run, same scratch store): the root project 206,302 -> 79,250 rows and `s-dev-composition` 183,817 -> 92,188, the live heap after the inference -0.46 / -0.13 GB (-0.19 / -0.13 GB after a collection), the peak resident set 5.93 -> 5.63 GB and 3.58 -> 3.17 GB; the project values are equal field by field to the ones of the direct walk. Oracle: a Python walk of its own copy of the tree after 22 recorded and 40 x 14 seeded edits of directories and files anywhere (`🚶️repository-walks`): the derived answer equals the full walk after every edit, and whenever every supporting row still holds the answer is the one the holders hold.

- **Cheap generations after an environment move (graph generations).** When the environment digest moves (a manifest, the lock file, a package name, an owner) the 4,019 owners keep their fragments and only get their edges again, and the 264 test owners are discovered again. Both are cheap now. The test discovery runs inside ONE validation round (`👁️observation` `withObservationRoundV1`): a stored fact or memoized computation is validated once per round instead of once per use (measured on a scratch copy of generation 547: 69 s -> 3-8 s wall, 138 -> 80 s CPU of the whole delta), and the command walk of the testing domain (about 20 thousand rows, the same for every owner) is a derived fact like the walks of the projects (`resolveCommandWalkV1`), so a test owner holds one row for it and an edit of a file of the walk that keeps its answer leaves all test owners valid. The edges of an owner that only gets its edges again are streamed (`🕸️dependency-edges` `createStreamingEdgesImplementation`): one owner at a time from the module facts of its published node, without reading or parsing a source (the edge discovery of that stretch: 18-48 s -> 2.6 s, the fragments rewrite included 20 s -> 18 s on a loaded host), equal to the full edge discovery owner by owner on the real graph; the import ownership is an ancestor lookup instead of a scan of every owner (`createRelativeImportResolverV1`), and the complete module facts are retained only for the owners that are inferred. The scripts of the closure read the provider ports of a module through an analysis fact (`providerPorts`), not through its bytes.
- **Cheap assembly of a covered generation (graph generations).** The walk of the workspace no longer reads every directory when the tree moved: the membership frontier keeps the entry stamp of all 79,000 directories, a run stats them and lists only the ones whose stamp moved plus the directories that appeared below them (`🕰️generations/🌿️frontier-walk`; on the real tree 146 directories instead of 79,140, 1.3 s instead of 7.7 s of cpu), and its answer equals a full walk (law on a real directory with seeded random edits, plus an independent Python walker). Verification opens the fact index as a lazy view (`openFactIndexV1`): the strings stay front coded in the document and become text when a row asks for them, facts are read at their offsets and a row becomes an object when it is first read, so checking a closure of 550 projects keeps about 230 MB live instead of 460 MB; a build still loads the index whole to patch it (`patchFactIndexV1` finds the stored twin of a new row by path and kind instead of building the identity of all 525,000 rows). The closure of a selection keeps only the target edges, the projects of the inputs and `dependsOn` of each fragment while it is computed (`sliceViewV1`), the environment reads the manifests of 3,750 projects 64 at a time, and a delta generation discovers only the test owners whose own facts are stale (`🕸️graph/🎯️test-owners`): every test project carries the facts of the shared command closure of the testing domain (computed once) followed by the facts of its own discovery, so an edit of one test directory discovers that owner again and nothing else.
- **Nested commands read the generation of their Original (`SEMIO_BUILD_GENERATION_PIN`).** A `repo run` that an orchestrated Bazel or semio action starts (the dashboard `build` runs `repo run …:compile`) answers to an Original run that already loaded and sealed a graph generation. The Original names that generation in one closed JSON pin (`🕰️generations/🧷️pin`: the digest of the manifest, the owning invocation and a verified stamp, the same bitmap as the coverage stamp, over exactly the projects it served) right after it pinned its graph; every descendant inherits it, and the caller's environment issuer drops it for every task that is not orchestrated. A nested command binds the pin only while its owner is an invocation it answers to and alive, and never in the Original itself. A bound pin is the graph of the command: the manifest is read by its digest, nothing is verified against the live tree, the builder custody and its queue are never entered and nothing is planned; the plan child of a nested loader reports the generation in a `<plan>.pin` sidecar so the nested loader hands the same pin on. The capacity grant was already handed down through `SEMIO_HOST_CAPACITY_GRANT` (inherited branch of the gate, no second queue). When the pinned generation was retired, belongs to another identity or lacks an owner of the closure the command needs, the command refuses in English and German (`graph: this command runs inside a build whose pinned graph generation N does not cover K of the projects it needs (…); it does not plan a graph of its own`) instead of planning its own graph. Only the build tools `bazel` and `semio` export the pin (Nx tasks do not pass the caller's environment issuer and keep their own planning), `watch` never does (its children re-plan on every change) and a pin older than four hours is not bound.
- **Generations.** Every build publishes an immutable generation: one content-addressed fragment per owner (its neutral node, its outgoing edges, its module facts) and one fact index in which every distinct observation row is stored once. The pointer `current.json` flips by a single compare-and-swap after all documents are written, so a reader always sees one complete generation and takes no lease. An edit dirties its own owner and the owners that consumed the same fact (a membership proof from directory stamps replaces the tree walk); only the dirty owners are inferred, in one call, so the shared import closure is walked once per build. Writers coalesce on one builder custody, and the holder returns a peer's generation when the work is already done. The facts lease is held only for the pointer swap (a test holds a 300 ms build for at least 290 ms under the build lease and under 100 ms under the facts lease). `repo show project <name>` expands only the closure of that project.
- **The fact index is binary** (live). The inverted fact index of a generation is a columnar binary document (`🕰️generations/🧮️fact-codec`, byte layout in its schema file), not JSON text: one front coded string table shared by all paths, kinds and fact strings, every distinct fact stored once with digests as raw bytes and numbers as varints or doubles, rows as three integer columns, classes as zigzag deltas. On the real workspace (516,788 rows) it is 20 % of the former canonical JSON (180.7 MB -> 36.0 MB expanded, 8.2 MB packed) and is decoded straight from the buffer in about a quarter of a second; the 512 MiB reader ceiling is unchanged and now far away (`graph: fact index of generation N: ... % of the 512 MB limit` is logged after every published generation). An independent Python encoder produces the same bytes and a Python decoder reads them; the storage closure reader scans the table strings instead of streaming JSON.
- **Policy.** `--graph-policy=fresh|current` (or `SEMIO_BUILD_GRAPH_POLICY`) applies to `graph` and `show`; task commands (`run`, `run-many`, `affected`, `exec`, `watch`) always use `fresh`.

| Policy | Serves | Waits |
|---|---|---|
| `fresh` (default) | a generation that verifies against the tree; the dirty owners are rebuilt first when something it needs is dirty | only for the builder, and only when something is dirty |
| `current` | the newest sealed generation at once, possibly older than your last edit | never |

- **Builds survive edits** (live). A build completes with the implementation it loaded. An edit of an implementation file (the graph and inference modules themselves) no longer aborts a running build with "Stale loaded ...; restart required": while a build is pinned, the 12 self-checks of that kind return and the inference keeps the revision it computed first, with no mid-build swap. The pinned scopes are the command door, an owned build invocation and the generation load; the strict path (baseline, reuse, selected graphs) is unchanged. The next fresh load refreshes only the projects that consumed the edited module: each fragment records a digest fact (path, SHA-256, size, modification time) for every module it consumed, where inert modules (the ledger) are consumed by nobody, node-shaping modules (project nodes, target policy, playground and component targets, generator outputs) by the valid projects and every other module by every project and the environment. The manifest of a generation carries the digest of the loaded implementation (25 graph files and 63 inference files); `current` serves any generation of the registry identity and says `stale` in English or German when its stamp differs from the reader's, `fresh` verifies the rows and refreshes exactly the consumers, and a reader that loaded an older implementation than the one on disk finds the newer generation verified and serves it. The first read of an authority datum (the owner registry, the graph declarations: policy and schemas) holds for the whole pinned invocation. Proof: 300 projects (30 unavailable) built while an editor rewrote a node module every 250 ms published in one pass within 1.2 times the quiet time plus 0.5 s, the next fresh load refreshed exactly the 270 valid projects and the following one nothing. The retirement of optional graph files, which held the graph image exclusively for the whole run, now works in exclusive windows of 15 s with rechecks every 64 files and releases the image between windows.
- **Oracle.** `SEMIO_BUILD_GRAPH_VERIFY=1` runs the unchanged full inference once more and names the first project whose node, edges, external nodes or named inputs differ. Use it after changing graph code.
- **Applying the generations.** The change invalidated every graph receipt and every project record once; the next graph build after it was cold and `repo tools setup nx` ran again afterwards.
- **A cold graph is the slowest command.** On 2026-10-20 the all-workspace inference of 3,735 projects ran at 6 projects per minute at host load 39 and at 33 per minute at load 21 to 27; it is bound by garbage collection of a 7 to 8 GB heap, not by leases or disk, so keep the host load below 25 while it runs. A pass publishes the snapshot it saw: a project that raced an edit is published and marked stale for the next generation, never restarted. Files that are not code (a JSON catalogue, a label, a schema) are recorded by shape and code files by their import analysis, so a body edit of a closure file no longer stales the roughly 670 native records (an import change still does); the closure revision is the digest of the path list; and the rows that thousands of records repeat are stored once as shared content-defined blocks (`np/<24 hex>.json`). The persisted per-project records survive a restart, but a restart repeats the pre-project phases (5 to 8 minutes) and every waiting command with it.

### 🗃️ Shared caches and budgets

Caches are shared where sharing is safe and private where a second writer would corrupt or serialize the first. Every shared cache is content addressed (a key is the hash of its inputs, an entry is written to a temporary name and renamed into place), so two agents that compute the same key at the same time at worst compute it twice.

| Cache | Shared by | Bounded by | Cleared or collected by |
|---|---|---|---|
| Dependency generations (`.cache/b/d`, `j/<key>`) | every command; a generation is immutable once its ready marker exists | partition `d`, 8 GiB; a sync retires generations that no plan and no binding names | `repo dependencies sync`; the retention rule below |
| Nx task cache (`nx/artifacts` in the rust partition) | all agents (concurrent writers of one hash are safe) | 3 GiB, declared in `nx.json` as `maxCacheSize` | `repo cache clear` |
| Bazel disk cache (`.cache/b/p/m/bazel/actions`) | all selections and all sessions | 40 percent of the products budget; entries older than 30 days dropped | `repo cache clear` |
| Bazel output bases | one per session slot (`s0`, `s1`, ...; a slot has one holder) | at most 6 build slots and 4 service slots, 6 generated packages per slot, 7 days | `repo cache clear` and the collector of slots |
| Cargo build state | a private lane per agent (`m/u/l/<id>`) plus one content-addressed unit cache (`m/u`) for all | the rust partition, 48 GiB | idle lanes after 24 hours; above 87.5 percent used a niced single-flight background collector frees down to 75 percent (incremental sets, then lanes, then objects, oldest first); between 75 and 87.5 percent its watcher still trims the lane, object and admission-memo shares once at its start and every 10 minutes, and the memos live at most 7 days; a command that finds less than one compile charge free collects first, and one that hits `No space left on device` collects and repeats once |
| Project graph (`.cache/b/g`) | every command | partition `g`, 6 GiB | `repo reset` |
| Test sandboxes | none (one per run, below the OS temporary directory) | removed on disposal; dead owners are reclaimed | the next sandbox creation |
| Remote cache | other machines and users | the server's own bound | the server |

The partitions and their capacities are described in Cache partitions.

**Cache ledger** (live). Every cache byte has exactly one home: a fixed partition, a nested area inside a partition with its own budget (`nx-artifacts` 3 GiB, `cargo-store` the remainder of the rust partition, `bazel-actions` 40 percent of products), or a budgeted area of a declared loose root (`repository` capped at 24 GiB, `native` at 256 MiB). Anything else is `retired` (a one-time rule in the ledger, removed after its quiet time; dead installs of old tools, the old lease, closure and preparation roots) or `undeclared` (no declared writer; collected after 14 days without use, native after 7). The runtime door `repoCacheDirectory` refuses an undeclared first segment with an English or German message, and a law scans the tracked sources for `⚡️cache` literals, so a new cache needs a ledger entry first.

```bash
repo cache status
repo cache gc --dry-run
repo cache gc --area=<id>,<id>
repo cache status --tasks --tool=bazel
```

- **`cache status`** prints the ledger: each home, its budget, its use (`at least` when the entry cap of the walk was hit) and what is collectable; it takes no lease, so it works while the dependency image is held. `--json` prints JSON and `--lang=en|de` selects the language.
- **`cache gc` collects by default.** Always run `--dry-run` first and read the list: it proposes whole dead installs below `.🧬semio/🦑️repo/⚡️cache/tools` (about 25 GiB) and age-eligible undeclared directories, and you must check that none of them is a live cache of a product.
- **Lock-aware.** Collection re-measures every unit right before removing it and never removes a unit that is a lease database whose exclusive transaction cannot be taken without waiting, that sits in a queue with a ticket of a living process, that lies in an area whose declared lease cannot be taken immediately, that was touched within the area's quiet time or that changed after the scan. Hard links count once and links are never followed. Removal is batched, cancellable (exit code 130) and reports progress. Areas that their own tool manages (Nx, Cargo, Bazel, the dashboard) are reported, not collected; test evidence is delegated to the same pruning call as `cache-prune`.
- **Nx toolkit store and Nx temp trees.** `repo cache gc --area=nx-tooling,nx-tmp` keeps the newest toolkit generations of the tools image (8; 3 under pressure of the machine budget or less than 2.5 GiB free) plus the current activation, every generation a live reader holds through its `nx-tooling:<identity>` lease and every one younger than 10 minutes, retires the rest oldest first (16 per run) inside the whole-image exclusive scope once the image is idle (bounded polling, no queue; `SEMIO_NX_TOOLING_IDLE_WAIT_MS`), and removes `nx/tmp/tmp-<pid>-*` trees whose process is gone after an hour; `--dry-run` plans, progress and cancellation as for every collection.
- **Not verified.** A live run, the `statfs` path of a mounted image (tests cover detached, directory and absent states), Linux, Windows, and Nx honouring `maxCacheSize` at run time.

**Dependency generations** (live). A writer runs in phases and holds only what each phase needs. `build` installs fresh scopes into new generations under a shared image lease plus one builder lease; `publish` switches the bindings and replaces the per-scope pointer atomically (the only window in which readers are refused, 400 ms in the test, waiting at most 15 minutes for readers to drain); `collect` removes unreferenced generations through a tombstone rename. Readers hold the bindings gate and a pin on the generation they read. A second `ensure` that finds the builder busy rechecks readiness every 5 s and returns as soon as the build made it unnecessary; four concurrent ensures start one build. Retention: an unreferenced generation is kept 6 hours after it was retired, the newest retired generation of a scope 24 hours (rollback target), both shrink to 0 while the image has less than 25 percent free, at most 16 are removed per run and a pinned generation never. One manifest edit builds one scope. Maintenance and admission (staged): collection holds only the shared image, with no build lease, no bindings gate and no whole-image hold, so a running build never blocks it; a generation goes only if no pointer's current one, no live binding and no pin holds it and the retention rule is due (pin, tombstone rename, delete), and the builder pins every plan generation before it creates or resumes it, which is what makes lease-free collection safe. Before every fresh scope the expected size (the size recorded in that scope's pointer, else the largest recorded, else 1 GiB, times 1.15) must fit the free bytes; if it does not, collection reclaims at once (with the pressure grace, which also frees rollback targets), the size is measured again, and if it still does not fit the sync refuses before any install with an English or German diagnostic (`admissionRefused`); published generations stay untouched and the next `ensure` resumes with the scopes already built. Until then a scope larger than the free space fails with `ENOSPC` without touching readers. Open: writing under shared custody has not run on a real image, whole-image maintenance that needs quiescence (reset, layout) still waits for a build, and there is no command yet to collect dependency generations by hand.

**In CI** (live). `.github/workflows/build-system.yml` is built around one value, `BUILD_TOOL` (default `bazel`; the dispatch input `tool` switches to `nx`), and every build step is a `repo` call that takes the tool from `--tool="$BUILD_TOOL"`: no step names an implementation, an installer, a flag or a cache path. The jobs are `plan` (the `affected` range of the pull request, push or dispatch), `smoke` (Linux, macOS and Windows: zero-touch start, `tools setup`, `dependencies sync --all` and a cache proof that stores once and serves the second run with the remote cache skipped), `affected` (builds and tests only the changed projects and their dependents, reports the hit rate and uploads the JUnit XML of every executed test) and `parity` (both tools select the same tasks for the range). The shared remote cache is configured only by repository settings: the variable `SEMIO_REMOTE_CACHE_ENDPOINT` (an `http(s)` origin of `repo cache serve` or any Bazel HTTP cache that also speaks the Nx cache API), the optional variable `SEMIO_REMOTE_CACHE_INSTANCE` and the secrets `SEMIO_REMOTE_CACHE_READ_TOKEN` and `SEMIO_REMOTE_CACHE_WRITE_TOKEN`; pull requests, forks and dispatches get read-only access without upload and the read token, only a push to the default branch gets `read-write` with upload and the write token. Without an endpoint the local task cache directory travels through the GitHub cache (key `disk-<tool>-<os>-<arch>-<sha>`, restored always, saved only by default-branch pushes). After every Bazel `test` step the adapter exports the `test.xml` of each label to the directory named by `SEMIO_BUILD_TEST_RESULTS_DIR`. The workflow has not run yet; likely first failures are `preflight` and `tools setup` on the Linux and Windows runners, and a core change selects almost every project (hours on a cold first run).

**Across machines.** The remote cache is the cross-machine layer (see Caching model and Remote cache). Hermetic actions have host-independent keys; the keys of today's host-state tasks contain the Bun path and the checkout root, so a hit across machines needs the same user and checkout path. The Cargo unit cache and the TypeScript declaration store are local to a machine; a remote layer for them is not defined.

### 🌡️ Host capacity

Nothing used to know what the machine could carry: every command started at once and each tool sized itself to the whole host, which ended in watchdog kernel panics on 2026-09-28, 2026-09-29 and 2026-10-20 under load. The host capacity governor is one admission domain per user: every heavy `repo` command (`run`, `run-many`, `affected`, `exec`, `watch`, `cargo`, `show`, `graph`, `dependencies`) is admitted before it starts, waits in a first-come-first-served queue (interactive before batch) when the host cannot carry it, and gets a grant that sets the parallelism of the selected tool. Light commands pass untouched. State: live.

```bash
repo capacity status
repo capacity run --memory=6144 -- repo tools exec bun -- test ./some.test.ts
repo capacity run --operation=dependencies -- repo dependencies sync --all
```

- **`capacity status`** prints the host sample (available memory, the floor, spare, pressure, swap, load, open files, attached images), every running grant with what it reserved and what it really holds, and every waiting request; `--json` prints JSON.
- **`capacity run`** puts any other heavy command (a test runner, an installer) through the same queue: `--memory=<megabytes>` reserves exactly that, `--operation=<name>` reserves the estimate of a catalogue operation, `--cpu=<cores>` and `--jobs=<n>` size the request. The command gets the grant in its environment and the grant is returned when it ends, even if the process dies.
- **Admission.** A request is admitted while the free memory stays above the floor (12.5 percent of host memory clamped to 1.5 to 4 GB: 2,048 MB on a 16 GB host, 4,096 MB on a 32 GB host), swap stays below 25 percent and swapping out below 64 MB/s, the load stays below 2 per core counting the cores of running grants, memory pressure is not `critical` (`warn` waits), the machine-wide open file table stays below 80 percent and fewer than 32 disk images are attached. The last three and raised pressure are soft: they are waited out and overridden after 120 s only when nothing of ours is running, because a leaked image never goes away by waiting. Memory, swap and critical pressure are never overridden. Estimates: test 1.5 GB, check 4 GB, generate 1 GB, build 2 GB, script 0.75 GB, service 2 GB; `show` and `graph` 5 GB, `dependencies` 6 GB. A request that does not fit gets fewer jobs (`reduced`); one larger than the whole budget runs alone on an idle machine.
- **Grants per tool.** Bazel receives `--local_resources=cpu=N`, `--local_resources=memory=MB` and `--jobs` (and resource sets per action mnemonic, so its scheduler books actions at their estimate), Nx receives `NX_PARALLEL` (it has no memory or core budget, so the mapping is approximate), Cargo receives `--parallelism`. Nested commands inherit the grant of their parent through the environment, so a task that calls `repo` again does not wait for itself.
- **Reading a wait.** A line such as `[capacity] waiting 42s for run: not enough free memory (need 3584 MB; 1200 MB spare above the 6553 MB floor, load 7.1; 2 running, 1 ahead)` names the reason (`memory`, `swap`, `swap-activity`, `pressure-critical`, `pressure-warn`, `files`, `attaches`, `load`, `ramp` or `queue-order`) and the queue position. The line is published every 5 s in English or German; Ctrl+C cancels the wait. The state lives in `~/Library/Caches/semio/host-capacity` (`$XDG_CACHE_HOME` or `~/.cache` on Linux, `%LOCALAPPDATA%` on Windows), shared by every checkout of the user; a grant whose process died or whose boot session ended is reclaimed.
- **Escape hatch.** `SEMIO_HOST_CAPACITY=off` switches the governor off and says so on every command. The governor admits, it does not kill; commands that run outside `repo` are governed only when wrapped in `repo capacity run`.
- **Fairness.** A reservation counts as unrealized, and in the budget at its full estimate (or at what the grant already holds, when that is more), for the time its class needs to reach its peak (`rampMilliseconds` of the grant, learned from when earlier runs of the class peaked, 5 min for an unknown class, between `memory.rampMilliseconds` and 30 min); afterwards the memory it really holds speaks for itself. At most `memory.maximumRamping` (2) reservations that were shrunk below the default by learning may grow towards their peak at once (reason `ramp`), so a wrong estimate overshoots with a bounded number of runs. Only completed runs teach a class; a failed or cancelled run only when its partial peak already exceeds the estimate. A refused request never holds back later requests that fit; it becomes a strict barrier only if finishing our own grants would admit it and it has waited 30 s or been overtaken 6 times, and foreign pressure alone never creates a barrier. A batch request older than 300 s ranks as interactive. The bound on starvation is the bypass time plus one short duration. State: live.
- **Platforms.** macOS reads `vm_stat` and `sysctl`, Linux `/proc/meminfo`, pressure stall information and the cgroup limits, Windows only free physical memory and busy-CPU load (no machine-wide file table, no pressure level). Only macOS was run; the other parsers are tested from fixtures.

### 🔒️ Leases, lock order and queues

Every shared resource is claimed through a lease: a SQLite file `<leaseDir>/<sha256 of the resource name>.sqlite`, plus a `.queue/` directory of tickets for the queued kind. Queued leases (`acquireQueuedResourceLease`) are served in arrival order and a writer is not starved by readers that arrive after it; the raw kind (`acquireResourceLease`) is polled and prefers readers. A waiter at position 2 or later prints a `lease-queue` line every 15 s that names the holder's process id (see Waiting for a store in the reference). The rest of this section is the contract for lock order, deadlocks and the status command (live).

**Lock order.** A resource name falls into one class; a class has a rank, a scope and an owner, all declared in the lock-order table `🧰️framework/🔨️modules/🏃️process/🔒️leases/🧭️order/🔣️policy.json` (a closed schema next to it, English and German messages). A task that holds a lease and asks for a lower rank is out of order. Unranked resources are rank 0 and never constrained.

| Rank | Class | Scope | Resources (examples) |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | compiler capacity | machine | `semio.compiler.cpu-picker/v1`, `semio.compiler.cpu/v1/<n>` |
| 2 | tool singletons | checkout | `nx-tooling-*`, `bazel-*` (metrics, snapshot restore, slots), `bun-*`, `rustup:*`, `cache-prune` |
| 2 | Cargo coordination | crate | `cargo:registry:*`, `cargo-preparation:<root>`, `cargo:source-admission` |
| 3 | graph | checkout | `nx:graph-pool:*`, `repository-graph-facts:<workspace>`, `build-graph-*` |
| 4 | namespaces | project | `rust-*`, `javascript-dependency-*`, `typescript-package-binding:*`, `native-snapshot*` |
| 5 | artifacts | path | `artifact:<path>` (shared reads, exclusive publish) |
| 6 | images | whole image | `native-fixed-image:<directory>` (exclusive for sync, install and walks, shared for readers) |

- **Rules.** *Out of order* is reported (`observe`, the shipped mode) and flips to a hard failure with one line of policy once `repo leases status` shows no violation during the end-to-end battery. *Hold-and-wait* fails a blocking request that has waited longer than 15 s while its task holds another lease exclusively; the message names the held lease, the blocking process, its pid and how long it holds. Shared holds, declared edges and try-locks are exempt. *Self-deadlock* (shared then exclusive on the same lease, or exclusive then anything unbounded) fails at once. *Cycle*: every waiter checks the wait-for graph every 500 ms and exactly one member of a cycle, the latest waiter, fails while the others proceed. `SEMIO_LEASE_ORDER=off|observe|enforce` overrides all four.
- **Waiting no longer blocks admission.** A raw acquire used to keep the shared directory-admission gate while it waited for a busy resource, which blocked every other acquire; it now takes the gate per attempt and waits outside it. `withResourceLeases` acquires in rank-then-name order through the fair queue, so three overlapping readers cannot starve a writer.
- **Retirement of idle leases.** A lease store keeps one SQLite file, one `.queue/` directory and one settled claim per resource and ticket, and none of them used to leave: the only sweep of idle databases ran inside exclusive image maintenance, which no run ever got while dozens of commands held the image, so the 63 MiB lease image filled to 84 % (1,671 databases, 1,936 queues, 28 abandoned nested test stores of 4 MiB each) and every opener swept claims once per second. The opener now runs an incremental sweep under the ordinary lease protocol (`sweepResourceLeaseStoreV1`, `🔒️leases/♻️retirement/🧹️sweep`): at most every 5 minutes, every 30 seconds while less than 20 % of the store is free (stamp `.retired`, shared by all processes), in slices of 64 (256 under pressure) candidates that each take the directory gate exclusively for a few milliseconds, persist their cursor and honour cancellation and the deadline between candidates. An entry goes only when nothing holds its database (an exclusive transaction succeeds), its queue directory is empty (a crashed requester's ticket is reaped first through its free claim), and neither file was used for 15 minutes (1 minute under pressure; acquire and release stamp the file's modification time once it is older than a minute, `touchResourceLeaseUseV1`). A returning acquirer recreates what was retired: the queue directory is created inside the shared claim catalog, which the sweep holds exclusively. A nested store (a lease directory below the lease directory, as killed test runs leave behind with their own 4 MiB headroom file) goes when every file in it is recognized, nothing in it was used for an hour and the kernel grants an exclusive transaction on all of its databases at once. Messages in English and German; a pressure sweep that retired something prints one `[repo leases]` line. The size of the lease image follows the ledger need `leases` (entries at their bytes, headroom file, three nested stores, one claim turn of floor; 64 MiB); the area is `derived`, so a larger image is born by an exclusive-ticket recreate that waits for every holder and has nothing to carry. Tests: `🔒️leases/♻️retirement/🧹️sweep/🧪️tests` (corpus with Ajv, an independent Python `sqlite3` oracle for the retirement prediction and for the acquire/release replay of racing sweeps, a SQLite model of the pressure rule) and the `leases` need in `💾️storage/🧾️ledger/🧪️tests/📏️needs`.
- **Standing, priority, yield and reclaim.** A lease holder now tells the others how it fares (schema-first: `🔒️leases/📈️standing/🧬️schema/🔣️.json`, tests `📈️standing/🧪️tests`). Every process publishes in its registry entry a *standing*: priority class, implementation identity (a digest of the declared format-stamp components it loaded plus its load time), the phase and counters of its latest progress line (`build:graph-phase:inference:120:566` becomes "inference: 120 of 566"; a trailing `:N` counts), and the time of its last progress. The time left comes from the rate of its own counter, else from the median of earlier holds of the same kind (`history.jsonl` next to the registry, resource family plus command kind); a hold far past that median is reported as "longer than usual". **Priority** is the arrival under which a ticket is filed: the real arrival moved earlier by 60 min for `interactive` (a person at a terminal outside an agent session), not at all for `agent` (the default) and later by 60 min for `background`; override with `SEMIO_LEASE_PRIORITY=interactive|agent|background`. Tickets are served in filed order, so a class overtakes another only inside that window and every request is overtaken by a bounded set: aging is the arrival time itself, nothing starves. Replaying tonight's shard-7 queue (30 waiters, services reconstructed from the 15 s queue notices) with six interactive rebuilds added: interactive mean wait 94 to 25 min, agents 86 to 57 min, background 90 to 108 min (worst 123 to 136 min); the same total work finishes at the same moment. **Freshness:** `repo leases status` judges every exclusive holder as `progressing`, `silent` (nothing reported for 1 min), `stuck` (20 min for the graph builder custody, 60 min elsewhere), `stale` (it loaded an older value of a format stamp that binds the resource than the newest process in flight, so its result would be discarded) or `unknown` (no live registry entry carries its standing; nothing is ever decided against it), with its code age, phase, counters, time left, yield request and the chain of invocations that started it. **Yield:** a waiter that loaded newer code and finds the exclusive holder stale on a stamp-bound resource files a request next to the registry; the holder polls it every 5 s, checks the claim itself (addressed to it, fresh, the resource held exclusively, an older and different value of a bound component) and then ends the way an outdated implementation ends (exit 75, the outermost command starts again with the current code); a healthy holder is never asked. **Reclaim** follows `SEMIO_LEASE_RECLAIM=report|request|terminate` (default `request`): `request` also asks a stuck holder to give the resource up; `terminate` additionally stops a stuck holder that ignored the request for 2 minutes and made no progress since, only after the operating system's start time of the pid equals the registered one, only for a process the caller may signal and never itself: SIGTERM, then SIGKILL after 15 s (the head only while its start time still matches, then every child recorded with its own start time before the first signal, so a stopped builder leaves no planner behind and a recycled pid is never signalled), logged in `reclaim.jsonl`; a refusal is said once per holder, not at every tick. A crashed holder releases its lease through the kernel lock as before. Waiters see all this in the queue notice, for example `Waiting for access to repository-graph-facts:<root>:shard:7 (exclusive): position 3 of 9, behind the request of process 69857 (exclusive, queued 6300 s ago) · holder: progressing, inference 120 of 566, about 34 min left`, and in German. The capacity queue reads the same priority class through `leasePriorityClassV1` (exported by the leases facade). **Entry points declare their class** schema-first in the MCP build surface (`🔌️mcp/🖼️assets/🏗️build-surface.json`, `priority`): the fan-out tools `test`, `run_many`, `affected` and the `run` subcommands of `incremental`, `conformance`, `units`, `bench` and `concurrency` export `SEMIO_LEASE_PRIORITY=background`, every other tool leaves the class to the child (`agent` inside an agent session), and a person at a terminal outside an agent session is `interactive`; a golden-vector law pins the environment of every call. **Liveness is work, and a yield is verified on disk:** a holder renews its progress clock every 30 s by what its tree really does (the watchdog's own CPU sampler over the descendants, or at least 200 ms of CPU of the process itself), so a holder compiling for an hour without a line of output is never `stuck` and never asked, while a holder whose whole tree idles ages into `stuck` as before; a stuck yield ends restartably (exit 75) like a stale one instead of failing for the person who ran it. A stale yield is accepted only when the holder re-reads the stamps from disk itself and finds a bound component that differs from the requester's claim AND moved since it loaded it, so a reverted edit never makes a healthy holder give up a build. **Cancelled waits do not hang:** an accepted continuation of a wait (`onWait`, progress publication) is joined for at most 300 ms after the wait ended, so the lease database never closes under a running callback, and a callback that never returns is abandoned with the cancellation reason and a notice in English and German (`⏳️wait`, `continuationDrainMilliseconds`).
- **For authors.** Take the narrowest resource that makes your step correct (per project, crate, generation or path, not per checkout); never hold an exclusive lease while you wait for another; when you introduce or split a key, add a class with its scope and owner to the table. Optional and separate: `SEMIO_LEASE_INHERITED` makes child processes count their parent's holds, which can make previously accepted parent-child nestings fail the self-deadlock and hold-and-wait rules, so it ships after one clean cycle.

```bash
repo leases status
repo leases status --json --violations=50
```

- **`leases status`** shows the holders (mode, class, rank, pid, hold seconds, command), the waiters (queue position, wait seconds, blockers), the wait-for edges, deadlock cycles with their victim, the recent order violations and the machine admission queue of the host capacity governor with each waiter's reason. Options: `--json`, `--all`, `--lease-directory=<path>`, `--violations=<n>`. It reads the per-user registry (`~/Library/Caches/semio/lease-registry`, `$XDG_CACHE_HOME` or `~/.cache`, `%LOCALAPPDATA%`; `SEMIO_LEASE_REGISTRY_DIRECTORY` overrides it; dead pids are pruned, heartbeat 60 s) and the queue tickets, so processes on older code still appear as holders and waiters, without names.
- **Limits.** Task attribution is per invocation, so parallel sub-tasks of one invocation count as one task; deadlock detection sees only processes on the new code; the queued `withResourceLeases` adds claim and queue file churn per acquisition; the compiler capacity picker still holds its lease while it polls cores, so a small request can wait behind an unsatisfiable `exact:N` request.
- **If a command waits today.** Read the `lease-queue` line for the holder's pid and `[capacity]` lines for the governor; both are queues, not hangs. `repo leases status` lists who holds what and who waits for whom.

### 🧵️ Sessions: Bazel slots, Cargo lanes and Nx pools

Each tool had one place where two agents in one folder serialized. This section says what happens today and what the staged sets change; each paragraph carries its state.

**Bazel server slots** (live). A session claims one slot `s0`, `s1`, ... with an exclusive lease; a slot owns one short output base and the generated packages of its selections, and there is exactly one holder per slot, so a server lock wait cannot happen.

- **Slots.** The number of build slots K comes from the host budget: K = clamp(floor((memory minus floor) times 30 percent divided by the resident size of a server), 1 to 6) and at most cores divided by 2; on a 32 GB host with 10 cores that is 3 (16 GB: 1, 128 GB: 6). `SEMIO_BAZEL_SERVERS=<n>` (1 to 99; anything else is refused) overrides it, and the server heap comes from the plan instead of a literal. A session started inside a Bazel action gets 4 overflow slots, so it never waits for the slot its parent holds.
- **Service slots.** A selection in which any task is `continuous` (a dev server, `serve`) holds a service slot `d0`, `d1`, ... for its lifetime (default 4, `SEMIO_BAZEL_SERVICES=<n>`), with its own fair queue; every other selection, including each rebuild cycle of `watch`, is a build, so a queue of waiting dev servers never delays a build and a running service is never rebuilt over. After the launch the server idles out after 120 s, so a pinned dev server costs a slot name and an output base on disk, not a JVM.
- **Affinity and waiting.** A slot remembers its last selection; a session prefers the slot whose last selection it is (a slot keeps the package and the plan record of its last selection only), then a never-used slot, then the least recently used, so an edit-run-edit loop keeps its warm server and two alternating selections never evict each other. When every slot is busy the head of a fair queue polls every 250 ms and prints `[bazel] all 3 Bazel server slots are in use; waiting for one (12 s)` every 5 s (English or German); it honours cancellation. A waiting session holds no store lease: the order is capacity, then slot, then storage.
- **Shared layers.** The disk cache, the repository cache and a new repository contents cache (`<products>/bazel/contents`, one fetch of every external repository for all output bases) are shared by all slots, and action keys do not depend on the output base or the package path (a fresh slot rebuilt the same content with one disk cache hit and no spawn). Restores into the checkout lock the tree they write (`bazel-restore:<path>`, ancestors shared) instead of the whole checkout.
- **Measured** with real Bazel 8.4.2 and three slots: three sessions on three disjoint targets (4 s action each) took 13.3 s and 12.7 s against 35.4 s and 36.5 s summed; a fourth session in a fresh slot had 0 spawns; with three service sessions pinned the builds took 15.2 s; the control (two commands forced into one output base) showed the lock wait. Not verified: Windows and Linux hosts, three servers on the real 134k-target graphs, a real `watch` or `serve` end to end.

**Cargo lanes and the unit cache** (live). A lane is the private target and build directory of one agent, `m/u/l/<id>/{t,b,m,v}`, so no two Cargo processes ever share a build directory.

- **Lanes.** The lane id is `SEMIO_CARGO_LANE`, else derived from the nearest agent-client ancestor process (claude, codex, cursor, windsurf, copilot, antigravity, droid, kiro: one lane per agent session), else `d`. A busy lane overflows into `<id>1` to `<id>31`, which start empty and fill from the unit cache. `--store=shared` means "lane plus shared unit cache"; `--store=isolated` is unchanged. All lanes live inside the issued rust image and are bounded, so the 926 GB of private targets cannot recur. The launcher creates the directories itself and `.cargo/config.toml` names the default lane.
- **Unit cache.** Cargo runs the repository's rustc wrapper, which keys each unit by the toolchain, the normalised command line, the SHA-256 of the root source and of every extern artifact, and then verifies the dependency file rustc wrote (every file it read, by content). A hit restores the outputs by reflink where the file system supports it (APFS, btrfs, XFS; a real copy elsewhere) and replays rustc's JSON messages; a miss compiles once, publishes content-addressed objects and the entry last. Units of the packages selected with `-p` keep `-C incremental` in the lane and are never published.
- **No deadlock, bounded concurrency.** The first process that misses a unit claims its key; the others wait for the finished entry (bounded to 30 minutes, a dead holder is replaced). Host-wide compile slots (`SEMIO_UNIT_CACHE_SLOTS`, default the core count) are served first in first out; the lock order is claim, slot, rustc. The writer custody of the image is shared and escalates to exclusive only to recover missing headroom; registry fetches coalesce per request; the admission result of an unchanged unit is remembered under stamps.
- **Disk.** Idle lanes (24 h) go first; with less than 10 percent of the image free, incremental sets, then lanes, then cache objects are removed, oldest first; the shares are objects 25, lanes 20 and incremental 10 percent, live lanes are never touched. The launcher runs this at most every 10 minutes after a lane is released.
- **Build scripts and component passes** (live). The compiled build script is replaced by a stand-in that keys a run by the SHA-256 of the real executable, the toolchain, its arguments and Cargo's per-package, feature, cfg, target, rustflags and `DEP_*` variables; a hit writes the recorded `OUT_DIR` and replays the recorded `cargo:` lines and warnings without starting the script, a miss runs it once across all lanes and records `OUT_DIR`, both streams and the facts (every `rerun-if-changed` path, every `rerun-if-env-changed` value, the package tree, with `*.rs` ignored when the script declares inputs). A script whose `OUT_DIR` holds links or a binary that embeds the lane path, an output over 256 MiB or a package over 4,096 files runs normally; the stand-in needs a POSIX host, and the launcher exports `SEMIO_UNIT_CACHE_BUILD_SCRIPTS=on`. Component passes (the per-generation builds) use the same wrapper chain: a hit tells the scheduler broker a `cached` unit with the recorded peak memory and CPU, so the unit list, the critical path and the provenance reports stay complete and the scheduling history ignores cached units. 
- **Front-end threads and concurrency of different crates** (live). Cargo authors `-Z threads=0` (every core of the host) for every rustc; the scheduler (`semio-build-scheduler`) hands rustc the admitted parallelism instead (`SEMIO_COMPILER_PARALLELISM`): 0 becomes the grant, a larger count is capped, a smaller one stays, so Cargo fingerprints and unit-cache keys keep the authored flag. Measured on one 2.7 MB crate: 1 thread 2.9 GB peak RSS, 4 threads 5.4 GB, 10 threads 5.2 GB at constant CPU work. Two builds of two different crates in two lanes took 56.8 s instead of the 115.6 s of one after the other (peak RSS 2.6 GB instead of 1.4 GB). The model `🔒️lease/🧪️tests/🔓️unit-lock-model` (two implementations, one corpus) shows why lanes are the answer: over every pair of process shapes on three units a shared directory deadlocks in 24 of 325 pairs, a whole-directory custody never but serialises all of them, a lane per process never and overlaps all, and a per-unit custody overlaps 78 of 325 and is sound only with a dirty estimate that never misses a unit, which Cargo's own freshness (build-script reads, environment, rebuilt dependencies) makes impossible to compute outside Cargo.
- **Measured** on a 26-crate wasm workspace with three concurrent sessions: 32 distinct units compiled once instead of about 70, and a fourth session was served 32 of 32 from the cache; the wall time stays dominated by build scripts. Limits: proc-macros that read files without `tracked_path` can serve a stale hit, Windows reports no process ancestry (lane `d` with overflow lanes), reflink falls back to copying on ext4 and NTFS. The deadlock of many Cargo processes on one fine-grain-locked build directory (12 observed) was not reproduced with three; it is removed by construction because no two processes share a directory.

**Nx** (live). A generation receipt binds the three graph files of the digest namespace to the graph digest, the plugin implementation, the Nx tooling and the identity files; when it holds, `NX_FORCE_REUSE_CACHED_GRAPH=true` and Nx re-reads the workspace files but not the graph. Measured on a stock Nx graph of 2,500 projects: 6 to 7 s cold, 0.7 to 1.5 s with the receipt (expected on the live tree: about 24 s to the first task instead of 210 to 350 s). The pool follows the order namespace, then pool: attaching an existing namespace takes no pool, a birth is a short pass that only try-locks the namespace, and eviction runs outside the pool under its own queue. All users hold the fixed cache shared, so a put no longer blocks restores. Two concurrent stock Nx runs over the same 50 hashes, then a put of 150 and a restore of 50, had no failures. Open: the graph file copy of a publication still holds the pool. Daemon policy (live): finite daemons (`run`) are capped at the smaller of 2 and a quarter of the host memory divided by 2 GiB, watch daemons at 6 (`SEMIO_NX_DAEMONS=<finite>[,<watch>]`, 0 turns them off, at most 16); every daemon holds a numbered slot lease `nx:daemon-slot:<root>:<kind>:<k>` for its whole life and refuses to start when its family is full; a run reuses the live daemon of its namespace, is admitted while fewer than the cap are live, and otherwise runs with `NX_DAEMON=false` (graph from the receipt, plain hashing); an explicit `NX_DAEMON` of the caller wins; finite daemons drain after `SEMIO_NX_DAEMON_IDLE_MS` (default 10 minutes) without an open request. Put slots (live): puts no longer take the exclusive compiler-writer custody; they run under shared custody inside one of 4 put slots (`SEMIO_NX_PUT_SLOTS`, 1 to 16) behind a first-in-first-out gate, with the headroom measured as free space minus the maximum artifact size of every other slot in use, so concurrent puts cannot overcommit the image, and they degrade to one at a time when it is tight; the put slots need the shared writer custody of the Cargo set.

**Dependencies** are described under Shared caches and budgets (dependency generations); **TypeScript** under Incremental development.

### 🧪️ Test sandbox

Tests of many agents run at the same time on one machine, so a test must not share anything mutable with another run. Before the sandbox, 21 sites in 12 test files used the generated folder of an old ticket as their scratch root inside the checkout, `repoTestArtifactEnvironment` defaulted to one fixed route in the real cache that every concurrent run of the same route shared, 19 tests used constant names below the temporary directory, and 10 tests probed a port, closed it and bound the number later, which races between agents. A killed run also left an attached disk image whose backing file lay inside the checkout, where nothing ever reclaimed it. State: live.

- **One API.** `createTestSandboxV1`, `withTestSandboxV1`, `testSandboxEnvironmentV1` and `testScratchRootV1` in `🧰️framework/🔨️modules/🏃️process/🧪️testing/🏖️sandbox` (re-exported by the package export `./test-execution`). A sandbox is a private root `<tmpdir>/st/<8 hex>` (mode 0700, length bounded) with `artifacts/<route>`, `tmp`, `images` and `cache`, and an `owner.json` whose heartbeat is refreshed every 30 s. The environment carries `SEMIO_TEST_SANDBOX`, `SEMIO_TEST_ARTIFACT_DIR`, `SEMIO_TEST_IMAGE_ROOT`, `SEMIO_TEST_CACHE_ROOT`, `SEMIO_TEST_CHECKOUT_ROOT`, `SEMIO_TEST_GUARD`, `SEMIO_TEST_GUARD_REPORT` and `TMPDIR`, `TMP` and `TEMP`. Nested runs adopt the parent's sandbox.
- **It is automatic.** `runBudgetedTestCommand`, the one spawn point of Bun, Node and Go test commands, runs every child inside a per-run sandbox, so `repo run <project>:test` needs nothing from you; with no configured `SEMIO_TEST_ARTIFACT_DIR` the artifact directory is the one of the run's sandbox. Cargo test commands go through the Cargo launcher and get only that store's own `TMPDIR` handling. Go tests were already hermetic (17 owners run under `sandbox.Run`, a temporary git repository as root and working directory).
- **Disposal and reclaim.** The sandbox is disposed by `dispose()`, at process exit and in Bun's `afterAll` (a `bun test` process ends without running exit handlers, so `afterAll` is the one that counts): images below the root are detached, ports are released, the directory is removed. The next sandbox creation (at most every 5 minutes) detaches the images of and removes every sandbox whose owner process is dead or whose heartbeat is older than 1 hour, and reclaims stale port leases. The root and `TMPDIR` lie below the scratch roots of the boot sweep, so an image that even this misses is detached by its 15-minute idle sweep.
- **Ports.** `reservePort()` takes an atomic lease directory per port in the range 49152 to 65000, shared by every sandbox of the machine, bind-checks it and releases it on disposal. Never listen on a literal number and never probe and rebind.
- **The law.** `🧪️testing/🏖️sandbox/🧪️tests/⚖️law` scans the test sources of every manifest-owned directory (23 rules in TypeScript, Go, Rust, Python and shell, with English and German messages): writes into the real checkout, fixed temporary names, `process.cwd()` writers, disk-image and kill commands on non-sandbox paths, fixed ports, probe-then-rebind. Its error findings must equal the ledger `🧪️testing/🏖️sandbox/🧪️tests/🧫️fixtures/📒️ledger.json` exactly: a new violator fails, and a fixed one fails until its row is removed, so the ledger only shrinks. 31 rows remain, each with its reason (scratch that must lie below the workspace, the vitest lane, Rust fixed-name tests, the mcp adapters that build into the shared binary cache). The TypeScript scanner and a separate Python reference produce the same 1,042 findings over the tree, and ripgrep marks the same lines for every line rule.
- **Runtime guard.** `SEMIO_TEST_GUARD=off|report|enforce` (default `off`) preloads a shim for `node:fs`, `node:fs/promises` and `node:child_process` imports of repository TypeScript. It refuses or records writes inside the real checkout (`.cache`, `.🧬semio`, `.git`, `node_modules` are protected; `.cache/b/o/m/tests` is allowed), `hdiutil` and `diskutil` verbs on non-sandbox paths, `pkill` and `killall`, and fixed ports that the sandbox did not lease. Rows are JSONL (schema `GuardReportRow`) and summarised when the sandbox is disposed. Run a sample with `SEMIO_TEST_GUARD=report repo run <project>:test` and read the report before turning enforcement on; the default stays `off` until the loader rewrite has run across the 2,175 suites.
- **Verified** with 16 sandbox tests (349 expectations: 12 ports across two sandboxes and three child processes all distinct, a SIGKILLed child with an attached image reclaimed by the next sweep, the guard in both modes) and the law; the five envelope-gated converted suites and the whole battery under `report` were not run, nor Windows and Linux.

- **Running a single file, declaring its birth, placing its scratch.** `bun test ./<repo-relative file>` from the checkout root decides: the root `bunfig.toml` preloads the runtime pin and the orphan guard, and a run from the test's own directory uses the ambient Bun and misreports. A file that fails bare with `Original parent Script process invocation required` is envelope-only: it declares its birth with `declareBuildTestEnvelopeV1({owner,maximumElapsedMilliseconds,capabilities})`, and the capabilities must equal its suite row (live, batch `envelope` of lane td-leases). A test that starts Cargo creates its scratch workspaces below a directory that no `.cargo/config.toml`, `Cargo.toml` or `rust-toolchain.toml` reaches (`cargoScratchBaseV1()`, live, batch `scratch` of lane td-ucache); graph laws need a short directory inside the checkout. The operator guide (`📓️build-guide.md`, "Tests and routing tables") has the rules and the reasons.

### 🪞️ Parity verification

Nx and Bazel must produce the same results from the same neutral graph. `repo verify build-parity` proves it per sample instead of by inspection (live). `verify` is a root command, not a build operation: it first runs `repo dependencies ensure`.

```bash
repo verify build-parity --plans-only
repo verify build-parity --only=area:framework
repo verify build-parity --samples=<file> --out=<directory> --keep --json
repo verify build-parity --recover=<directory>
repo inputs @teaching/architecture-course:test
```

- **What one sample does.** The dry-run task graph of both tools and the closure (Bazel `cquery` against the planner); then `repo --tool=nx|bazel run <target>` fresh (`--skip-cache`) and restored from the cache; the declared outputs of each tool captured into separate roots and compared byte for byte (kind, mode, link target, SHA-256); exit codes, failed tasks and test status; the Nx `inputs` against the inputs and environment of Bazel's spawns (read from its execution log); and the cache restore of each tool cold against restored.
- **Only a rule explains a difference.** `🏗️build/🪞️parity/🔣️rules.json` holds 8 normalisations, each with a justification in English and German and its evidence, and the report counts how often each applied. Everything else is a divergence (35 codes, each with a help text) and the command exits non-zero. 27 samples in 6 areas (`🏗️build/🪞️parity/🔣️samples.json`: `framework`, `s-plugins`, `hub`, `teaching`, `mit-bestand`, `semio-tech`) are authored; `--only=<id>` or `--only=area:<area>` (comma separated) selects, `--samples=<file>` replaces them.
- **Safe for your tree.** Declared output roots are set aside before each run and restored in a `finally` (also on Ctrl+C); tracked roots are copied, untracked ones moved; `--recover=<directory>` restores from the journal after a crash. Progress is published per sample, tool and phase and every step is a cancellation checkpoint.
- **Reports.** `report.json`, `report.en.md` and `report.de.md` land in the evidence directory (`--out`, kept with `--keep`); `--lang=en|de` selects the help language. The Bazel side records its evidence (execution log, build events, closure) only when `SEMIO_BUILD_EVIDENCE_DIR` is set, which the verifier sets for its own Bazel runs.
- **`repo inputs <project>:<target>[:<configuration>]`** prints what Nx hashes for one task (files, environment, runtime values, dependency outputs, external packages) as JSON. Bazel does not implement it: its inputs are the spawn inputs in the parity evidence.
- **Not yet verified.** The real-tool answers: it was tested with fixtures, a fake `repo`, and Bazel evidence tests; the first live runs (the course build, the semio-tech play build, the mit-bestand demonstrator release build, a compact report print) are open. A heavy sample such as the demonstrator release build can exceed the 3,600 s sample limit. Run one process at a time; the host capacity governor applies to the child runs.

### 🥾️ Recovery after a reboot or a crash

A reboot, a kernel panic or a killed run leaves state behind: publication intents of a killed publisher, queue tickets of dead processes, disk images of finished tests, grants of the host capacity governor and device numbers that no longer mean what they meant. The boot sweep (live) recovers all of it at the next `repo` command, with progress and cancellation, and never blocks the command if a recovery step fails. You do not run a recovery command.

1. **Run the command you wanted.** The first partition open of the process runs the sweep. Its steps publish `boot-recovery:<stage>:<done>/<total>:<target>` progress lines; a failed step is reported as `boot-recovery:failed:<reason>` and the command continues; Ctrl+C cancels the sweep and the command.
2. **What the sweep does.**

| Leftover | Recovery |
|---|---|
| `.publication/intent.json` or `receipt.json` of a killed publisher (every command refused with "Pending Native image publication must settle") | a pure plan (rolled back, completed before the crash, torn, empty) executed under the exclusive image lease the opener already takes; ambiguous or unknown residue is refused with its reason and never deleted |
| queue tickets and claim databases of dead processes in `.cache/b/c` | tickets of an earlier boot are retired one by one, only after the repository's own exclusive claim-database proof; tickets of a dead process in the same boot are reaped by the next waiter behind them; once per boot, recorded in a ledger |
| disk images of finished or killed tests (the scratch-image class) and images whose backing file vanished (macOS) | an image under a scratch root (`/private/tmp`, `/private/var/folders`, `/var/tmp`, `/tmp`, `$TMPDIR`, `$SEMIO_TEST_ARTIFACT_DIR`), outside this workspace, idle for 15 minutes and with its own lease provably free is detached without force; a busy volume is skipped; workspace and foreign images are never touched; at most one sweep per 5 minutes |
| device numbers stored in receipts (they change after a reboot or a parent image re-attach) | no longer persisted truth: inode, mode, size and platform stay exact and devices are compared only through their pairing; image receipts are rebound to the new boot session without rewriting them |
| grants of the capacity governor of an earlier boot or of a dead process | reclaimed when the owner dies or the boot session changes |
| sandboxes and port leases of dead test runs | reclaimed by the next sandbox creation (dead owner, or heartbeat older than 1 hour) |

3. **What the sweep leaves.** Funded compiler-state children (an Nx namespace) with a reserved row and no commit stay until their owner resumes them; stale Bazel and Nx server files (`server.pid.txt`, cookies) are left to the tools' own stale-server handling; a reboot inside a live Bazel or Nx server session is not repaired by the sweep.
4. **Check afterwards.** `repo capacity status` lists no grant of the previous boot; `repo dependencies ensure` installs only what is missing. For the one refusal that still names a state ("Exact canonical persistent Native publication required …") and for inactive Graph or Nx caches, follow Recovery in the reference: use the dashboard control that owns the operation.
5. **Never** detach an image by hand, force an unmount, edit or move a receipt, or delete a directory below `.cache/b`. Cache partitions, Bazel output bases and the Cargo store are derived and safe to remove only when nothing runs and only through their owning controls (`repo cache clear`, the dashboard).

Verified in copies of the tree with real `hdiutil` (an abandoned and a fresh scratch image, a SIGKILL before each state-changing call and every torn write of the publication ceremony, the directory swap, a record transaction and the ledger append: recovery reaches the old or the new receipt and the second run is clean); the sweep measured 17 ms for the roster and 10 ms for a no-op. Not verified: a live drill after a real reboot, Linux and Windows (the boot epoch parsers are tested for all three platforms from fixtures).

### 🛫️ The native launcher and the runtime registry

`repo` is a native program, not a script that needs a JavaScript runtime installed by hand. It is written in Go with the standard library only, finds the checkout root, reads the runtime registry, provisions the pinned runtime when it is missing and hands over to it. State: launcher live, runtime seam staged.

```bash
repo --version
repo run <project>:<target>
repo ./some-script.ts arg1 arg2
repo --watch --cwd <directory> ./some-script.ts
```

- **Hand-over.** On macOS, Linux and the devcontainer the launcher replaces itself with the runtime process (same process id, same signals, same exit code); on Windows it starts a child with the console, forwards interrupts and returns the exit code. The directory of the runtime and the directory of the launcher lead the `PATH` of every child, so a nested `repo` or runtime call resolves the pinned one.
- **The registry is the swap point.** `🧰️framework/🛍️products/🦑️repo/🔨️modules/🚀️launcher/🗂️registry/🔣️.json` (a schema next to it, contract 1) lists runtimes: version, executable names, probe, https download base, launch options and environment, and one distribution per platform, architecture and libc with size and SHA-256. The entry names the selected runtime; `SEMIO_RUNTIME=<name>` overrides it. The launcher code knows nothing else about any runtime, and a law compares the registry with the existing pin files.
- **Provisioning.** The launcher looks first in the tools partition (`.cache/b/t/m/<runtime>/<sha>`, what `repo tools setup` writes) and then in the user cache (`SEMIO_RUNTIME_CACHE`, else the operating system's cache directory under `semio/runtimes`). When the runtime is missing it takes a lock file (peers wait, one download), downloads from the registry base or from `SEMIO_RUNTIME_MIRROR` (https or loopback http only), checks size and SHA-256 before unpacking, extracts with bounds, probes the version, publishes by atomic rename, prints progress on stderr and leaves nothing behind when you press Ctrl+C. It writes only the user cache: the tools partition is a mounted image that only the storage layer accounts for. With a warm cache a start costs 0.13 s in total.
- **Routes.** One planner, shared by the launcher and the full CLI: the build verbs are derived from the operations catalogue (no hand-written list), the workspace verbs (`dev mcp stdio os`), and the script route `repo [--watch] [--cwd <dir>] ./x.ts args`. The stand-alone launcher refuses the `mcp` and domain verbs with a hint to run `repo run repo-mcp:install`. A resident service gets the first chance at a plain `run|test|run-many` (see The resident service).
- **One command form.** Manifests, scripts and generated text say `repo ./x.ts` instead of `repo ./x.ts`, and a script's first line is `#!/usr/bin/env repo`. A generated wave rewrites 874 manifests (4,578 command strings, five left for review), 724 shebangs, the JSON process records and the help texts; two checks (`--check`) are the law that nothing names the runtime again.
- **`repo --version`** prints the launcher and contract version, every registered runtime (pinned version, and the path when it is provisioned; it never fetches), and the version of the implementation, in English and German. When no language can be resolved it prints both.
- **Install.** `bash 🧰️framework/🛍️products/🦑️repo/🔨️modules/🚀️launcher/📥️install/🐚️.sh [directory]` on macOS, Linux and in the devcontainer, `powershell -File 🧰️framework/🛍️products/🦑️repo/🔨️modules/🚀️launcher/📥️install/🔵️.ps1 [-Destination directory]` on Windows; neither needs Go or a JavaScript runtime installed: the installer finds the pinned Go (on the `PATH`, in the tools partition or in the user cache) or downloads it from the pins of the bootstrap table (https only, SHA-256 checked before it is unpacked, progress, Ctrl+C leaves nothing; `SEMIO_GO_MIRROR` names an https or loopback mirror) and builds the launcher from the checkout. On a bare machine the existing bootstrap `setup` calls it and installs Node, rustup and uv; nothing installs Bun or Go by hand.
- **rustc.** `.cargo/config.toml` and the compiler ingress policy name `repo` as the rustc wrapper (`repo <script> args`). Every rustc call therefore pays one launcher start (0.13 s with the runtime start included); the resident path is the way to make that cheaper.

**The runtime seam** (staged). Production code reaches the runtime only through the facade `🧰️framework/🔨️modules/⚙️runtime`: files, globbing, TOML, JSONC, YAML, process spawning, serving, SQLite, semver, source transpiling, bundling, native bindings, loader hooks and the test API. Two adapters implement it: the Bun adapter and a Node adapter (bundling through esbuild behind our interface, FFI through a provider port, loader hooks through `module.registerHooks`). One thin test API runs the same file under `bun test` and `node --test`. The law finds no `Bun` global, no program word `bun`, no `bun:*` specifier and no `versions.bun` in production code outside the directories named `🍞️bun`: 138 findings and a ledger of 76 tolerated exceptions became 0 findings and no ledger. Every runtime error comes from an English and German catalogue (57 ids). Every family entry point of the facade imports in under 2 ms (it was about 8 ms); the adapter loads on the first member use. Open: Node has no sandbox-guard preload yet, FFI on Node needs a provider addon, and 272 shebangs, help strings and 22 data records that name `bun` belong to the launcher wave.

**Verified** with 27 Go tests (registry, routes, provisioning integrity, one download for four concurrent starts, cancellation, English and German texts) and the real pinned runtime from a loopback mirror (the extracted executable equals an independent `shasum`); the runtime facade with 33 tests under both runners, 50 existing suites with identical pass and fail counts before and after, and a type check without new errors. Not verified: Windows and Linux, a real download from the publisher, the whole manifest wave, PowerShell and Docker.

### 🧰️ The tool registry and the direct-spawn law

No code starts an external program on its own. Every tool the repository uses (25 of them: `repo`, bun, node, bazel, nx, cargo with rustc, rustfmt, rustdoc and clippy, rustup, go, uv, python, dotnet, cmake, ninja, tsc, vite, vitest, playwright, tectonic, wasm-opt, jco, wasm-pack, hdiutil, docker, git, gh) is one row of a schema-first registry, and every start goes through one process API. State: registry staged, law staged.

```bash
repo tools list
repo tools verify --only=go,uv
repo tools exec --light git -- --version
repo tools exec --cwd <directory> cargo -- --version
repo verify tool-spawns --report
```

- **A row** names the tool, its kind and capabilities, the executables it owns (one owner each), its platforms, its pin, how it is provisioned, where it is located (an environment override `SEMIO_TOOL_<ID>`, the running runtime, a content-addressed provisioned directory, a package script, the `PATH` and well-known directories, in that order), how it is probed, the one adapter module that may start it raw, the globs allowed to call it, its environment rules and whether it waits in the host capacity queue. Texts exist in English and German.
- **Pins are pointers, never copies**: Go, uv, rustup, node and the .NET installer point into the bootstrap pin rows, bun, bazel, wasm-opt and tectonic into their own release tables, nx and tsc into the tools `package.json`, vite, vitest, playwright and jco into the root `package.json`, cargo into the `rust-toolchain.toml` channel, python into `requires-python`, cmake into the preset minimum. A pin is `pinned`, `constrained`, `floating`, `system` or `internal`, and a floating tool states its gap.
- **`repo tools list [--json]`** prints each tool with its pin, where it was found and its command. **`repo tools verify [--only=<ids>]`** resolves and probes every tool and reports missing ones and differences from the pin (exit code 1); on the primary host it reported real drift: bun 1.3.14 against the pin 1.4.2, go 1.25.0 against 1.26.9, uv 0.11.15 against 0.11.6, and tectonic and wasm-opt not provisioned. **`repo tools exec [--cwd <dir>] [--light] <tool> -- <args>`** runs a tool with the registry's environment, waits in the capacity queue unless `--light`, forwards Ctrl+C and returns the tool's exit code.
- **One process API in three languages**: `runToolV1` (TypeScript), `RunTool` (Go), `run_tool` (Rust). Options carry cwd, environment, signal, timeout, capture and a declared caller; progress shows the phases admitting, running and finished; errors are typed (`unknown-tool`, `tool-missing`, `caller-refused`, `cancelled`, `failed`) with English and German text. The old funnels (`runOwnedCommand`, `captureOwnedCommandV1`, `runCmd`, Go `ExecCommand`) route a registered command name through the registry, so hundreds of existing call sites moved without being touched.
- **Sanitized environment.** IDE and debugger injections (`NODE_OPTIONS`, `VSCODE_*`, `CURSOR_*`, `ELECTRON_RUN_AS_NODE`, `JAVA_TOOL_OPTIONS`, `npm_config_*`) are always dropped; allowlist tools keep only the base and their own variables; registry values win (`GOCACHE` and `PLAYWRIGHT_BROWSERS_PATH` live under the shared cache, `UV_PYTHON_DOWNLOADS=never`, dotnet telemetry off); every run is marked `SEMIO_TOOL_RUN=<id>`. Code that deliberately passed `NODE_OPTIONS` to a bare command must pass it to `runToolV1` as an explicit value.
- **The law** `repo verify tool-spawns [--report] [--json] [--strict] [--prune [--accept-new]]` scans TypeScript, Rust, Go, Python, shell and PowerShell sources and the commands declared in manifests. A registered tool started outside its adapter is a finding (`raw-spawn`), a manifest that declares more commands for a tool than before is a finding (`declared-grew`), and a call `runToolV1("id")` from a caller the row does not allow or for an unknown id is a finding. Production has no ledger and no tolerance: raw spawns of registered tools went from 207 to 0. Test code has a ratcheted ledger with one English and German reason (589 files, 1,134 spawns) that can only shrink (`--prune`; new rows need `--prune --accept-new`).
- **Runtime guard.** `SEMIO_TOOL_GUARD=refuse|warn|off` checks the declared caller and hooks `Bun.spawn`, `Bun.spawnSync` and `Bun.$`; named imports of `node:child_process` are linked before any hook can run, so the static law covers those. `off` switches the guard, the funnel routing and the caller check off together.
